Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I am Dr. Smith. Could you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello, Dr. Smith. Lately I keep hearing things that no one else seems to hear, and it’s starting to really bother me at work and at home.\n\nDoctor: Besides hearing these voices, have you also been experiencing periods where your mood is extremely elevated, or alternatively, very low and hopeless for a significant amount of time?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really experienced those kinds of extreme mood swings, like feeling super high or deeply hopeless. It's more that I just feel flat and indifferent, and I've really lacked the motivation to do much of anything lately.\n\nDoctor: Besides the voices and feeling indifferent, have you noticed any decline in your ability to take care of yourself, organize your thoughts, or communicate clearly with others?\n\nPatient: Yes, communicating has become really hard because it feels like too much effort to get the words out, so I mostly give short answers or stay quiet. I've also had moments where I freeze up or feel this strong urge to tap and line up items at the register, which makes organizing my actions feel slow and disrupted.\n\nDoctor: Have these voices and the difficulty with your thoughts been present consistently for at least six months, and do the voices ever comment on your actions or argue with each other?\n\nPatient: The voice and the freezing at the register have actually only been a steady problem for about three months, though my lack of motivation and social withdrawal have been building for over two years. The voice does comment on what I'm doing—like saying \"You messed that up\"—but it's just a single low male voice, so there aren't multiple voices arguing with each other.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that your thoughts are being broadcast out loud for others to hear, or that someone is putting thoughts directly into your mind that aren't your own?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced anything like that. I've never felt like my thoughts were being broadcasted for others to hear, nor have I felt like anyone was putting thoughts into my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any strong, unshakable beliefs that someone is plotting against you, spying on you, or that there is a specific hidden meaning behind everyday events?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel like anyone is plotting against me or spying on me. My main concerns are really just the voice I hear and those moments where I freeze up. I don't think there is any hidden meaning behind everyday events.\n\nDoctor: Did these experiences with the voice and the freezing start suddenly over the course of a few days, or did they develop more gradually over several weeks?\n\nPatient: They definitely developed more gradually over time rather than starting suddenly. While the voice and the freezing at the register became a steady, daily problem over the last three months, the broader changes in my motivation and behavior have actually been slowly building up for over two years.\n\nDoctor: Besides the auditory hallucinations and the freezing episodes, have you noticed any other unusual movements or physical postures that you seem unable to control or that feel repetitive?\n\nPatient: Yes, I get this uncontrollable urge to tap items three times before I can bag them at the register. Other times, I feel like I have to line them up in a very precise pattern first. It happens even when there's a line forming, and I know it looks really strange to others.\n\nDoctor: When you perform these tapping and lining-up rituals, do you feel like you are doing them to prevent something bad from happening or to reduce anxiety, or does it feel more like an automatic, mechanical movement that you simply cannot stop?"

While the voice and the freezing at the register became a steady, daily problem over the last three months, the broader changes in my motivation and behavior have actually been slowly building up for over two years.\n\nDoctor: Besides the auditory hallucinations and the freezing episodes, have you noticed any other unusual movements or physical postures that you seem unable to control or that feel repetitive?\n\nPatient: Yes, I get this uncontrollable urge to tap items three times before I can bag them at the register. Other times, I feel like I have to line them up in a very precise pattern first. It happens even when there's a line forming, and I know it looks really strange to others.\n\nDoctor: When you perform these tapping and lining-up rituals, do you feel like you are doing them to prevent something bad from happening or to reduce anxiety, or does it feel more like an automatic, mechanical movement that you simply cannot stop?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, and I haven't really thought about it in those terms. It doesn't feel like I'm trying to prevent something bad from happening, but more like an overwhelming, strange urge to tap and line things up that is just very hard to resist.\n\nDoctor: When you are performing these tapping and lining-up rituals, do you feel that they are an intrusive, unwanted behavior that you are trying to resist, or do they feel like a natural, even appropriate, way for you to interact with your environment?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don't really try to fight or resist the urge; it just feels like something I have to do in the moment before I can move on. Even though I know it looks strange to customers and holds up the line, it feels completely necessary to do before I can bag the items.

I've never felt like my thoughts were being broadcasted for others to hear, nor have I felt like anyone was putting thoughts into my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any strong, unshakable beliefs that someone is plotting against you, spying on you, or that there is a specific hidden meaning behind everyday events?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel like anyone is plotting against me or spying on me. My main concerns are really just the voice I hear and those moments where I freeze up. I don't think there is any hidden meaning behind everyday events.\n\nDoctor: Did these experiences with the voice and the freezing start suddenly over the course of a few days, or did they develop more gradually over several weeks?\n\nPatient: They definitely developed more gradually over time rather than starting suddenly. While the voice and the freezing at the register became a steady, daily problem over the last three months, the broader changes in my motivation and behavior have actually been slowly building up for over two years.\n\nDoctor: Besides the auditory hallucinations and the freezing episodes, have you noticed any other unusual movements or physical postures that you seem unable to control or that feel repetitive?\n\nPatient: Yes, I get this uncontrollable urge to tap items three times before I can bag them at the register. Other times, I feel like I have to line them up in a very precise pattern first. It happens even when there's a line forming, and I know it looks really strange to others.\n\nDoctor: When you perform these tapping and lining-up rituals, do you feel like you are doing them to prevent something bad from happening or to reduce anxiety, or does it feel more like an automatic, mechanical movement that you simply cannot stop?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, and I haven't really thought about it in those terms. It doesn't feel like I'm trying to prevent something bad from happening, but more like an overwhelming, strange urge to tap and line things up that is just very hard to resist.\n\nDoctor: When you are performing these tapping and lining-up rituals, do you feel that they are an intrusive, unwanted behavior that you are trying to resist, or do they feel like a natural, even appropriate, way for you to interact with your environment?\n\nPatient: I don't really try to fight or resist the urge; it just feels like something I have to do in the moment before I can move on. Even though I know it looks strange to customers and holds up the line, it feels completely necessary to do before I can bag the items.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your need to tap and line up items is a personal quirk or a way of expressing your unique personality, or do you feel that these actions are becoming a strange, disorganized disruption to your daily life?\n\nPatient: No, it definitely doesn't feel like a personal quirk or a way of expressing myself; it just feels like a strange, disruptive urge. It has become a real problem at work because it holds up the line, and it has actually led to me getting write-ups from my manager."
  }

I've stopped caring about things I used to love, like playing my bass, and I barely speak unless someone asks me a direct question because it takes too much effort to get the words out. This lack of interest and difficulty connecting with others has been going on for over two years now."

F20.2 (catatonic schizophrenia) is added because the 'freezing' and repetitive, non-anxiety-driven ritualistic movements are suggestive of catatonic behavior. Lately I keep hearing things that no one else seems to hear, and it’s starting to really bother me at work and at home.\n\nDoctor: Besides hearing these voices, have you also been experiencing periods where your mood is extremely elevated, or alternatively, very low and hopeless for a significant amount of time?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really experienced those kinds of extreme mood swings, like feeling super high or deeply hopeless. It's more that I just feel flat and indifferent, and I've really lacked the motivation to do much of anything lately.\n\nDoctor: Besides the voices and feeling indifferent, have you noticed any decline in your ability to take care of yourself, organize your thoughts, or communicate clearly with others?\n\nPatient: Yes, communicating has become really hard because it feels like too much effort to get the words out, so I mostly give short answers or stay quiet. The voice does comment on what I'm doing—like saying \"You messed that up\"—but it's just a single low male voice, so there aren't multiple voices arguing with each other.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that your thoughts are being broadcast out loud for others to hear, or that someone is putting thoughts directly into your mind that aren't your own?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced anything like that. I've never felt like my thoughts were being broadcasted for others to hear, nor have I felt like anyone was putting thoughts into my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any strong, unshakable beliefs that someone is plotting against you, spying on you, or that there is a specific hidden meaning behind everyday events?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel like anyone is plotting against me or spying on me. My main concerns are really just the voice I hear and those moments where I freeze up. I don't think there is any hidden meaning behind everyday events.\n\nDoctor: Did these experiences with the voice and the freezing start suddenly over the course of a few days, or did they develop more gradually over several weeks?\n\nPatient: They definitely developed more gradually over time rather than starting suddenly. While the voice and the freezing at the register became a steady, daily problem over the last three months, the broader changes in my motivation and behavior have actually been slowly building up for over two years.\n\nDoctor: Besides the auditory hallucinations and the freezing episodes, have you noticed any other unusual movements or physical postures that you seem unable to control or that feel repetitive?\n\nPatient: Yes, I get this uncontrollable urge to tap items three times before I can bag them at the register. Other times, I feel like I have to line them up in a very precise pattern first. It happens even when there's a line forming, and I know it looks really strange to others.\n\nDoctor: When you perform these tapping and lining-up rituals, do you feel like you are doing them to prevent something bad from happening or to reduce anxiety, or does it feel more like an automatic, mechanical movement that you simply cannot stop?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, and I haven't really thought about it in those terms. It doesn't feel like I'm trying to prevent something bad from happening, but more like an overwhelming, strange urge to tap and line things up that is just very hard to resist.\n\nDoctor: When you are performing these tapping and lining-up rituals, do you feel that they are an intrusive, unwanted behavior that you are trying to resist, or do they feel like a natural, even appropriate, way for you to interact with your environment?\n\nPatient: I don't really try to fight or resist the urge; it just feels like something I have to do in the moment before I can move on. Even though I know it looks strange to customers and holds up the line, it feels completely necessary to do before I can bag the items.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your need to tap and line up items is a personal quirk or a way of expressing your unique personality, or do you feel that these actions are becoming a strange, disorganized disruption to your daily life?\n\nPatient: No, it definitely doesn't feel like a personal quirk or a way of expressing myself; it just feels like a strange, disruptive urge. It has become a real problem at work because it holds up the line, and it has actually led to me getting write-ups from my manager.\n\nDoctor: When you exhibit these repetitive movements and freezing, do you experience a loss of emotional expression, such as a lack of facial movement or a monotone voice, that persists even when you are not performing these rituals?\n\nPatient: Yes, even when I'm not freezing or tapping, I feel a general flatness and lack the energy to express myself. I've stopped caring about things I used to love, like playing my bass, and I barely speak unless someone asks me a direct question because it takes too much effort to get the words out. This lack of interest and difficulty connecting with others has been going on for over two years now.\n\nDoctor: When you experience these periods of freezing or repetitive movements, do you remain completely unresponsive to people talking to you, or can you still hear and understand what is happening around you?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure how responsive I am during those freezing moments, because I completely lose track of time. It feels like only a few seconds to me, but then I \"snap back\" and see customers waving or asking if I'm okay, so I realize it was much longer. I'm really not sure if I can actually hear them while it's happening or if I just completely zone out."

Lately I keep hearing things that no one else seems to hear, and it’s starting to really bother me at work and at home.\n\nDoctor: Besides hearing these voices, have you also been experiencing periods where your mood is extremely elevated, or alternatively, very low and hopeless for a significant amount of time?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really experienced those kinds of extreme mood swings, like feeling super high or deeply hopeless. It's more that I just feel flat and indifferent, and I've really lacked the motivation to do much of anything lately.\n\nDoctor: Besides the voices and feeling indifferent, have you noticed any decline in your ability to take care of yourself, organize your thoughts, or communicate clearly with others?\n\nPatient: Yes, communicating has become really hard because it feels like too much effort to get the words out, so I mostly give short answers or stay quiet. I've also had moments where I freeze up or feel this strong urge to tap and line up items at the register, which makes organizing my actions feel slow and disrupted.\n\nDoctor: Have these voices and the difficulty with your thoughts been present consistently for at least six months, and do the voices ever comment on your actions or argue with each other?\n\nPatient: The voice and the freezing at the register have actually only been a steady problem for about three months, though my lack of motivation and social withdrawal have been building for over two years. I've never felt like my thoughts were being broadcasted for others to hear, nor have I felt like anyone was putting thoughts into my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any strong, unshakable beliefs that someone is plotting against you, spying on you, or that there is a specific hidden meaning behind everyday events?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel like anyone is plotting against me or spying on me. I don't think there is any hidden meaning behind everyday events.\n\nDoctor: Did these experiences with the voice and the freezing start suddenly over the course of a few days, or did they develop more gradually over several weeks?\n\nPatient: They definitely developed more gradually over time rather than starting suddenly. While the voice and the freezing at the register became a steady, daily problem over the last three months, the broader changes in my motivation and behavior have actually been slowly building up for over two years.\n\nDoctor: Besides the auditory hallucinations and the freezing episodes, have you noticed any other unusual movements or physical postures that you seem unable to control or that feel repetitive?\n\nPatient: Yes, I get this uncontrollable urge to tap items three times before I can bag them at the register. Other times, I feel like I have to line them up in a very precise pattern first. It happens even when there's a line forming, and I know it looks really strange to others.\n\nDoctor: When you perform these tapping and lining-up rituals, do you feel like you are doing them to prevent something bad from happening or to reduce anxiety, or does it feel more like an automatic, mechanical movement that you simply cannot stop?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, and I haven't really thought about it in those terms. It doesn't feel like I'm trying to prevent something bad from happening, but more like an overwhelming, strange urge to tap and line things up that is just very hard to resist.\n\nDoctor: When you are performing these tapping and lining-up rituals, do you feel that they are an intrusive, unwanted behavior that you are trying to resist, or do they feel like a natural, even appropriate, way for you to interact with your environment?\n\nPatient: I don't really try to fight or resist the urge; it just feels like something I have to do in the moment before I can move on. Even though I know it looks strange to customers and holds up the line, it feels completely necessary to do before I can bag the items.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your need to tap and line up items is a personal quirk or a way of expressing your unique personality, or do you feel that these actions are becoming a strange, disorganized disruption to your daily life?\n\nPatient: No, it definitely doesn't feel like a personal quirk or a way of expressing myself; it just feels like a strange, disruptive urge. It has become a real problem at work because it holds up the line, and it has actually led to me getting write-ups from my manager.\n\nDoctor: When you exhibit these repetitive movements and freezing, do you experience a loss of emotional expression, such as a lack of facial movement or a monotone voice, that persists even when you are not performing these rituals?\n\nPatient: Yes, even when I'm not freezing or tapping, I feel a general flatness and lack the energy to express myself. I've stopped caring about things I used to love, like playing my bass, and I barely speak unless someone asks me a direct question because it takes too much effort to get the words out. This lack of interest and difficulty connecting with others has been going on for over two years now.\n\nDoctor: When you experience these periods of freezing or repetitive movements, do you remain completely unresponsive to people talking to you, or can you still hear and understand what is happening around you?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure how responsive I am during those freezing moments, because I completely lose track of time. It feels like only a few seconds to me, but then I \"snap back\" and see customers waving or asking if I'm okay, so I realize it was much longer. I'm really not sure if I can actually hear them while it's happening or if I just completely zone out.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes where you freeze or perform rituals, do you find yourself adopting and holding unusual or rigid body postures for extended periods, or do you simply find it difficult to initiate movement?\n\nPatient: It's not really about holding rigid postures or having trouble starting to move. It's more that I suddenly freeze or pause right in the middle of an action, like holding a can in midair or staring at the barcode scanner. To me, it only feels like a few seconds, even though it's long enough for customers to notice and ask if I'm okay."

To me, it only feels like a few seconds, even though it's long enough for customers to notice and ask if I'm okay.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find that you automatically repeat or echo words spoken to you by others, or do you find yourself mimicking the movements or gestures of the people around you?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I do that. I haven't noticed myself repeating what other people say or copying their movements."

Even though I know it looks strange to customers and holds up the line, it feels completely necessary to do before I can bag the items.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your need to tap and line up items is a personal quirk or a way of expressing your unique personality, or do you feel that these actions are becoming a strange, disorganized disruption to your daily life?\n\nPatient: No, it definitely doesn't feel like a personal quirk or a way of expressing myself; it just feels like a strange, disruptive urge. It has become a real problem at work because it holds up the line, and it has actually led to me getting write-ups from my manager.\n\nDoctor: When you exhibit these repetitive movements and freezing, do you experience a loss of emotional expression, such as a lack of facial movement or a monotone voice, that persists even when you are not performing these rituals?\n\nPatient: Yes, even when I'm not freezing or tapping, I feel a general flatness and lack the energy to express myself. I've stopped caring about things I used to love, like playing my bass, and I barely speak unless someone asks me a direct question because it takes too much effort to get the words out. This lack of interest and difficulty connecting with others has been going on for over two years now.\n\nDoctor: When you experience these periods of freezing or repetitive movements, do you remain completely unresponsive to people talking to you, or can you still hear and understand what is happening around you?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure how responsive I am during those freezing moments, because I completely lose track of time. It feels like only a few seconds to me, but then I \"snap back\" and see customers waving or asking if I'm okay, so I realize it was much longer. I'm really not sure if I can actually hear them while it's happening or if I just completely zone out.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes where you freeze or perform rituals, do you find yourself adopting and holding unusual or rigid body postures for extended periods, or do you simply find it difficult to initiate movement?\n\nPatient: It's not really about holding rigid postures or having trouble starting to move. It's more that I suddenly freeze or pause right in the middle of an action, like holding a can in midair or staring at the barcode scanner. To me, it only feels like a few seconds, even though it's long enough for customers to notice and ask if I'm okay.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find that you automatically repeat or echo words spoken to you by others, or do you find yourself mimicking the movements or gestures of the people around you?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I do that. I haven't noticed myself repeating what other people say or copying their movements.\n\nDoctor: When you are in these states of freezing or performing your rituals, do you find yourself following instructions from others, or do you feel a sense of resistance or 'negativism' where you feel compelled to do the opposite of what is asked of you?\n\nPatient: I don't really feel like I'm trying to resist anyone or follow specific instructions during those moments. I just feel completely zoned out or 'stuck' in my own head, and I'm not really sure how else to describe it."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========

The 'freezing' and ritualistic behaviors are complex; while they share features with OCD, the patient describes them as disorganized/automatic rather than ego-dystonic or anxiety-driven, pointing toward catatonic phenomena (F20.2) or disorganized behavior (F20.1). Given the prominence of the negative symptoms and the nature of the motor disturbances, F20.0 (Paranoid) is now included as a differential due to the auditory hallucinations, though the clinical picture leans heavily toward the disorganized or catatonic subtypes.", "is_final": false}

Could you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello, Dr. Smith. Lately I keep hearing things that no one else seems to hear, and it’s starting to really bother me at work and at home.\n\nDoctor: Besides hearing these voices, have you also been experiencing periods where your mood is extremely elevated, or alternatively, very low and hopeless for a significant amount of time?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really experienced those kinds of extreme mood swings, like feeling super high or deeply hopeless. It's more that I just feel flat and indifferent, and I've really lacked the motivation to do much of anything lately.\n\nDoctor: Besides the voices and feeling indifferent, have you noticed any decline in your ability to take care of yourself, organize your thoughts, or communicate clearly with others?\n\nPatient: Yes, communicating has become really hard because it feels like too much effort to get the words out, so I mostly give short answers or stay quiet. I've also had moments where I freeze up or feel this strong urge to tap and line up items at the register, which makes organizing my actions feel slow and disrupted.\n\nDoctor: Have these voices and the difficulty with your thoughts been present consistently for at least six months, and do the voices ever comment on your actions or argue with each other?\n\nPatient: The voice and the freezing at the register have actually only been a steady problem for about three months, though my lack of motivation and social withdrawal have been building for over two years. The voice does comment on what I'm doing—like saying \"You messed that up\"—but it's just a single low male voice, so there aren't multiple voices arguing with each other.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that your thoughts are being broadcast out loud for others to hear, or that someone is putting thoughts directly into your mind that aren't your own?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced anything like that. I've never felt like my thoughts were being broadcasted for others to hear, nor have I felt like anyone was putting thoughts into my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any strong, unshakable beliefs that someone is plotting against you, spying on you, or that there is a specific hidden meaning behind everyday events?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel like anyone is plotting against me or spying on me. My main concerns are really just the voice I hear and those moments where I freeze up. I don't think there is any hidden meaning behind everyday events.\n\nDoctor: Did these experiences with the voice and the freezing start suddenly over the course of a few days, or did they develop more gradually over several weeks?\n\nPatient: They definitely developed more gradually over time rather than starting suddenly. While the voice and the freezing at the register became a steady, daily problem over the last three months, the broader changes in my motivation and behavior have actually been slowly building up for over two years.\n\nDoctor: Besides the auditory hallucinations and the freezing episodes, have you noticed any other unusual movements or physical postures that you seem unable to control or that feel repetitive?\n\nPatient: Yes, I get this uncontrollable urge to tap items three times before I can bag them at the register. Other times, I feel like I have to line them up in a very precise pattern first. It happens even when there's a line forming, and I know it looks really strange to others.\n\nDoctor: When you perform these tapping and lining-up rituals, do you feel like you are doing them to prevent something bad from happening or to reduce anxiety, or does it feel more like an automatic, mechanical movement that you simply cannot stop?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, and I haven't really thought about it in those terms. It doesn't feel like I'm trying to prevent something bad from happening, but more like an overwhelming, strange urge to tap and line things up that is just very hard to resist.\n\nDoctor: When you are performing these tapping and lining-up rituals, do you feel that they are an intrusive, unwanted behavior that you are trying to resist, or do they feel like a natural, even appropriate, way for you to interact with your environment?\n\nPatient: I don't really try to fight or resist the urge; it just feels like something I have to do in the moment before I can move on. Even though I know it looks strange to customers and holds up the line, it feels completely necessary to do before I can bag the items.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your need to tap and line up items is a personal quirk or a way of expressing your unique personality, or do you feel that these actions are becoming a strange, disorganized disruption to your daily life?\n\nPatient: No, it definitely doesn't feel like a personal quirk or a way of expressing myself; it just feels like a strange, disruptive urge. It has become a real problem at work because it holds up the line, and it has actually led to me getting write-ups from my manager.\n\nDoctor: When you exhibit these repetitive movements and freezing, do you experience a loss of emotional expression, such as a lack of facial movement or a monotone voice, that persists even when you are not performing these rituals?\n\nPatient: Yes, even when I'm not freezing or tapping, I feel a general flatness and lack the energy to express myself. I've stopped caring about things I used to love, like playing my bass, and I barely speak unless someone asks me a direct question because it takes too much effort to get the words out. This lack of interest and difficulty connecting with others has been going on for over two years now.\n\nDoctor: When you experience these periods of freezing or repetitive movements, do you remain completely unresponsive to people talking to you, or can you still hear and understand what is happening around you?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure how responsive I am during those freezing moments, because I completely lose track of time. It feels like only a few seconds to me, but then I \"snap back\" and see customers waving or asking if I'm okay, so I realize it was much longer. I'm really not sure if I can actually hear them while it's happening or if I just completely zone out.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes where you freeze or perform rituals, do you find yourself adopting and holding unusual or rigid body postures for extended periods, or do you simply find it difficult to initiate movement?\n\nPatient: It's not really about holding rigid postures or having trouble starting to move. It's more that I suddenly freeze or pause right in the middle of an action, like holding a can in midair or staring at the barcode scanner. To me, it only feels like a few seconds, even though it's long enough for customers to notice and ask if I'm okay.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find that you automatically repeat or echo words spoken to you by others, or do you find yourself mimicking the movements or gestures of the people around you?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I do that. I haven't noticed myself repeating what other people say or copying their movements.\n\nDoctor: When you are in these states of freezing or performing your rituals, do you find yourself following instructions from others, or do you feel a sense of resistance or 'negativism' where you feel compelled to do the opposite of what is asked of you?\n\nPatient: I don't really feel like I'm trying to resist anyone or follow specific instructions during those moments. I just feel completely zoned out or 'stuck' in my own head, and I'm not really sure how else to describe it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find that you maintain a rigid, fixed body position for a long time, or do you have periods where you show a significant lack of response to your environment, such as not reacting to physical touch or loud sounds?\n\nPatient: I do experience moments where I catch myself standing perfectly still, frozen mid-action like holding a can in midair or staring at the barcode scanner. It only feels like a few seconds to me, but it lasts long enough that customers have to wave or ask if I'm okay to get me to snap out of it. I don't think I hold rigid positions for a really long time beyond those freezing moments."

Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt. Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking. Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.
